- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### GPG: A Simple and Strong Reinforcement Learning Baseline for Model Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02546v1)
- **Authors**: Xiangxiang Chu, Hailang Huang, Xiao Zhang, Fei Wei, Yong Wang
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement Learning (RL) can directly enhance the reasoning capabilities of large language models without extensive reliance on Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT). In this work, we revisit the traditional Policy Gradient (PG) mechanism and propose a minimalist RL approach termed Group Policy Gradient (GPG). Unlike conventional methods, GPG directly optimize the original RL objective, thus obviating the need for surrogate loss functions. As illustrated in our paper, by eliminating both the critic and reference models, and avoiding KL divergence constraints, our approach significantly simplifies the training process when compared to Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). Our approach achieves superior performance without relying on auxiliary techniques or adjustments. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method not only reduces computational costs but also consistently outperforms GRPO across various unimodal and multimodal tasks. Our code is available at https://github.com/AMAP-ML/GPG.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Group Policy Gradient (GPG), a reinforcement learning (RL) approach designed to directly enhance the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) without extensive reliance on supervised fine-tuning (SFT).  GPG revisits the traditional Policy Gradient (PG) mechanism and proposes a minimalist approach that directly optimizes the original RL objective, eliminating the need for surrogate loss functions, critic models, and reference models, as found in methods like Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). The authors demonstrate that GPG simplifies the training process, reduces computational costs, and consistently outperforms GRPO across various unimodal and multimodal tasks. The paper provides experimental results on math reasoning, visual reasoning, classification, and grounding tasks, showcasing GPG's superior performance and robustness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The key novelty lies in the *simplification* of the RL training process for LLMs. While the concept of policy gradient methods is not new, GPG distinguishes itself by removing components typically considered necessary for stable training, namely the critic model, reference model, KL-divergence constraints and surrogate losses, common in PPO-based methods.  The core idea of using group-level rewards for normalization to reduce variance is valuable.  While the concept of a group normalization is reminiscent of batch normalization techniques in supervised learning, adapting it within a policy gradient framework, especially in the context of LLM reasoning, constitutes a novel contribution.

However, it's crucial to evaluate the depth of this novelty.  The fundamental PG principle is well-established. The improvement hinges on the effectiveness of the *group reward normalization*.  The authors *do* provide empirical evidence that this normalization strategy works effectively across various tasks, but they don't offer extensive theoretical justification about why it consistently offers improvements. The ablation studies help to confirm the individual contribution of the group normalization but could benefit from more analytical support. Further, some recent work has looked into removing the critic [27], and this paper acknowledges that direction as well.

**Significance:**

The significance of this work comes from its potential to make RL-based LLM reasoning training more accessible and efficient. Reducing computational cost without sacrificing performance is a valuable goal, particularly as LLMs continue to grow in size.  The empirical results presented are compelling, showing clear improvements over GRPO, a relevant baseline.

The paper also contributes by open-sourcing their code, allowing for easier adoption and further research by the community. However, as admitted by the authors, the experiments were not conducted on extremely large models due to limited computational budget which limits the broader applicability until validated at larger scales.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear and Concise Writing:** The paper is well-written and easy to understand.
*   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experiments demonstrate consistent improvements over a relevant baseline (GRPO).
*   **Ablation Studies:** The inclusion of ablation studies (group size, reward normalization) helps to isolate the effects of the proposed techniques.
*   **Open-Sourced Code:** Promotes reproducibility and further research.
*   **Computational Efficiency:** It points out the computational burdens and tries to lighten them while maintaining the effectiveness of the approach.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Theoretical Justification:** The paper provides a compelling empirical argument, but lacks a more in-depth theoretical explanation for the effectiveness of group reward normalization.
*   **Lack of Scale Validation:** Experiments on larger models are needed to assess the scalability of the method.  This is a self-admitted limitation.
*   **Simplistic Baselines?** The baselines are based on GRPO which also has been criticized by some for its complexity. Perhaps experiments against newer, simpler, efficient fine-tuning method would have added more credibility.

**Potential Impact:**

The paper could potentially influence the development of more efficient RL-based LLM reasoning training techniques. The simplicity and effectiveness of GPG could lead to its adoption by researchers and practitioners working on LLM alignment and reasoning. It may also inspire further research into alternative reward normalization techniques and simplification of RL algorithms for LLMs.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

A score of 7 reflects the paper's significant empirical contribution and promising simplification of RL for LLMs. The paper demonstrates a clear performance advantage over the GRPO baseline on a variety of tasks and shows a simplification that warrants exploration. However, the limited theoretical analysis and lack of validation on very large models temper the score. The degree of novelty could be debated, as it leans heavily on simplification, and the impact relies on GPG's scalability which is yet to be thoroughly examined. Nevertheless, it showcases a practical, effective simplification that advances the field.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Exploring Individual Factors in the Adoption of LLMs for Specific Software Engineering Tasks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02553v1)
- **Authors**: Stefano Lambiase, Gemma Catolino, Fabio Palomba, Filomena Ferrucci, Daniel Russo
- **Abstract**: The advent of Large Language Models (LLMs) is transforming software development, significantly enhancing software engineering processes. Research has explored their role within development teams, focusing on specific tasks such as artifact generation, decision-making support, and information retrieval. Despite the growing body of work on LLMs in software engineering, most studies have centered on broad adoption trends, neglecting the nuanced relationship between individual cognitive and behavioral factors and their impact on task-specific adoption. While factors such as perceived effort and performance expectancy have been explored at a general level, their influence on distinct software engineering tasks remains underexamined. This gap hinders the development of tailored LLM-based systems (e.g., Generative AI Agents) that align with engineers' specific needs and limits the ability of team leaders to devise effective strategies for fostering LLM adoption in targeted workflows. This study bridges this gap by surveying N=188 software engineers to test the relationship between individual attributes related to technology adoption and LLM adoption across five key tasks, using structural equation modeling (SEM). The Unified Theory of Acceptance and Use of Technology (UTAUT2) was applied to characterize individual adoption behaviors. The findings reveal that task-specific adoption is influenced by distinct factors, some of which negatively impact adoption when considered in isolation, underscoring the complexity of LLM integration in software engineering. To support effective adoption, this article provides actionable recommendations, such as seamlessly integrating LLMs into existing development environments and encouraging peer-driven knowledge sharing to enhance information retrieval.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates individual factors influencing software engineers' adoption of Large Language Models (LLMs) for specific software engineering tasks. It addresses a gap in existing research, which primarily focuses on overall LLM adoption trends rather than task-specific adoption drivers. Using a survey of 188 software engineers and Structural Equation Modeling (SEM) based on the UTAUT2 framework, the study examines relationships between individual attributes (performance expectancy, effort expectancy, social influence, etc.) and the use of LLMs for tasks like artifact manipulation, alternative generation, information retrieval, decision support, and training. The findings reveal that task-specific adoption is influenced by distinct factors, some of which can have negative impacts when considered in isolation, highlighting the complex nature of LLM integration in SE. The paper concludes with actionable recommendations for promoting effective LLM adoption, such as integrating them into development environments and encouraging peer-driven knowledge sharing.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its task-specific analysis of LLM adoption. While prior research has examined LLM adoption in SE, this study breaks down the analysis to understand how different individual and behavioral factors drive the use of LLMs for *specific* tasks. This granularity is a crucial step towards developing tailored LLM-based tools and effective adoption strategies. It moves beyond generic adoption studies. It also considers the UTAUT2 framework and applied it to LLMs in SE, which helps it validate the approach of considering this framework for this case.

**Significance:** The findings have significant implications for both researchers and practitioners.

*   **Researchers:** The study identifies specific areas where focused tool development could be beneficial, guiding future research on task-specific LLM applications.
*   **Practitioners:** The actionable recommendations offer practical guidance to organizations aiming to integrate LLMs into their software development processes effectively. The insights into negative impacts of certain factors (like over-reliance on external support discouraging LLM use for decision-making) are valuable for optimizing adoption strategies.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Research Question:** The research question is well-defined and addressed systematically.
*   **Rigorous Methodology:** The use of a validated questionnaire, a substantial sample size (N=188), and SEM provides a robust and statistically sound analysis. The use of UTAUT2, a well-established framework, adds credibility.
*   **Actionable Recommendations:** The paper concludes with concrete recommendations that are directly applicable to real-world scenarios.
*   **Addresses a Gap:** The paper successfully identifies and addresses a gap in the literature.
*   **Replicability:** The authors provide all the material for replication, including the online appendices.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Self-Reported Data:** Reliance on self-reported survey data introduces potential biases (social desirability, recall accuracy). Complementing this with behavioral data (interaction logs) would strengthen future research.
*   **Generalizability:** While the sample is geographically diverse, it's still a snapshot of the current landscape. The findings may not be generalizable to all software development contexts or across time as LLM technology evolves.
*   **Limited Scope of Factors:** The study focuses primarily on factors within the UTAUT2 framework. Other potentially relevant factors (e.g., company culture, project complexity, specific skills required) are not explicitly examined.  The limited R² value for the training use case suggests that there might be additional factors not accounted for by the current model.
*   **Cross-Sectional Design:** As a cross-sectional study, the authors cannot establish causality.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper represents a solid contribution to the field of software engineering by providing a granular understanding of LLM adoption at the task level. While it has some limitations related to data collection and scope, the strengths of the methodology, the clarity of the findings, and the actionable recommendations warrant a positive evaluation. The novelty is considerable, and it does have the potential to shape tool design and adoption strategies. I am assigning a score of **7**. The paper is not ground-breaking but addresses a significant gap with reasonable methods and implications.  The limited explanatory power for the training use case and the potential for biases in self-reported data are important drawbacks that prevent a higher score.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Leveraging LLM For Synchronizing Information Across Multilingual Tables
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02559v1)
- **Authors**: Siddharth Khincha, Tushar Kataria, Ankita Anand, Dan Roth, Vivek Gupta
- **Abstract**: The vast amount of online information today poses challenges for non-English speakers, as much of it is concentrated in high-resource languages such as English and French. Wikipedia reflects this imbalance, with content in low-resource languages frequently outdated or incomplete. Recent research has sought to improve cross-language synchronization of Wikipedia tables using rule-based methods. These approaches can be effective, but they struggle with complexity and generalization. This paper explores large language models (LLMs) for multilingual information synchronization, using zero-shot prompting as a scalable solution. We introduce the Information Updation dataset, simulating the real-world process of updating outdated Wikipedia tables, and evaluate LLM performance. Our findings reveal that single-prompt approaches often produce suboptimal results, prompting us to introduce a task decomposition strategy that enhances coherence and accuracy. Our proposed method outperforms existing baselines, particularly in Information Updation (1.79%) and Information Addition (20.58%), highlighting the model strength in dynamically updating and enriching data across architectures
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, "Leveraging LLM For Synchronizing Information Across Multilingual Tables," focusing on its novelty and significance.

**Summary**

This paper investigates the use of large language models (LLMs) for automating the synchronization of information across multilingual tables, specifically Wikipedia infoboxes. The authors address the problem of outdated or incomplete information in low-resource languages compared to high-resource languages. They introduce a new "Information Updation" dataset to simulate the real-world process of updating Wikipedia tables. The paper then evaluates the performance of LLMs, particularly GPT-4, on this dataset using zero-shot prompting. The authors find that a single-prompt approach is suboptimal and propose a task decomposition strategy to improve coherence and accuracy. Their proposed method outperforms existing rule-based baselines in information updation and addition, demonstrating the potential of LLMs for dynamically updating and enriching data across languages. The authors also introduce new evaluation metrics tailored for the information updation task.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Problem Relevance:** The problem addressed is highly relevant. Addressing the information gap in low-resource languages is important for ensuring equitable access to knowledge.
    *   **Novel Dataset:** The creation of the "Information Updation" dataset is a significant contribution. It provides a valuable benchmark for evaluating information synchronization methods, and its focus on simulating real-world Wikipedia update scenarios enhances its practical value.
    *   **Task Decomposition Approach:** The task decomposition strategy is a key strength. The paper's finding that breaking down the complex task of information synchronization into smaller, manageable subtasks using multiple prompts leads to improved performance is well justified by the experimental results.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides thorough experimental results, comparing the proposed approach against strong baselines, including rule-based methods and direct prompting. Ablation studies further validate the effectiveness of different components of the task decomposition strategy.
    *   **Novel Evaluation Metrics:**  The authors recognize the limitations of existing evaluation methods and propose new metrics tailored to the information updation task.  These metrics provide a more nuanced understanding of model performance.
    *   **Detailed Error Analysis:** The paper includes a detailed error analysis that provides insights into the limitations of current state-of-the-art LLM models.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited LLM Diversity:** The experimental evaluation primarily focuses on GPT-4 (and other models via API). Exploring a wider range of open-source LLMs could provide a more comprehensive understanding of the capabilities and limitations of different models. Even the use of LLaMA is done via API calls, which reduces the transparency regarding the specific version/training data.
    *   **Computational Cost:** The paper mentions that the computational demands of their approach could pose challenges for broader scalability, particularly in resource-constrained environments.  While acknowledged, the paper lacks concrete proposals for mitigating this issue.
    *   **Limited Human Evaluation:** While the paper acknowledges the superiority of human evaluation, only 100 random samples have been tested this way. The majority of evaluations are made using LLMs, which could affect the accuracy of the conclusions.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The key novelty lies in the application of LLMs *with a specific task decomposition strategy* to the problem of multilingual information synchronization in semi-structured tables. While LLMs have been used in various NLP tasks, their systematic application with a carefully designed prompting strategy to address this specific challenge is a novel contribution.

    *   The significance stems from the potential to automate the process of updating and enriching information in low-resource languages, thereby reducing the information gap and promoting more equitable access to knowledge.

    * The work has good potential to improve the state-of-the-art in automated knowledge base construction and maintenance, particularly in multilingual settings. The new dataset and evaluation metrics can contribute to future research in this area.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper could influence future research by providing a new benchmark dataset and evaluation metrics for information synchronization tasks.
    *   The proposed task decomposition strategy could serve as a template for applying LLMs to other complex NLP problems.
    *   The findings could encourage further exploration of LLMs for automating knowledge base construction and maintenance.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a well-defined problem, a novel dataset, a valuable task decomposition strategy, thorough experimental results, and new evaluation metrics. The contributions are significant, and the paper has the potential to influence future research in the field.

However, the limitations regarding LLM diversity, limited human evaluation, and computational cost prevent it from achieving a higher score. While the paper acknowledges the issue with few human samples compared to the total amount of data, and explains that it is difficult due to time and costs restrictions, it could still impact on the validity of the conclusions.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Language Models reach higher Agreement than Humans in Historical Interpretation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02572v1)
- **Authors**: Fabio Celli, Georgios Spathulas
- **Abstract**: This paper compares historical annotations by humans and Large Language Models. The findings reveal that both exhibit some cultural bias, but Large Language Models achieve a higher consensus on the interpretation of historical facts from short texts. While humans tend to disagree on the basis of their personal biases, Large Models disagree when they skip information or produce hallucinations. These findings have significant implications for digital humanities, enabling large-scale annotation and quantitative analysis of historical data. This offers new educational and research opportunities to explore historical interpretations from different Language Models, fostering critical thinking about bias.
- **Summary**: **Summary:**

This paper explores the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) for historical data interpretation by comparing their annotations of historical events with those made by humans. The research focuses on two annotation tasks: identifying phases of the secular cycle (using 5 labels) and phases of the big cycle (using 3 labels). The authors aim to determine whether LLMs exhibit historical or cultural biases, achieve higher consensus than humans, and what types of errors each make. They find that while both humans and LLMs can exhibit biases, LLMs tend to achieve higher consensus in the secular cycle annotation task. Humans, on the other hand, show a more nuanced agreement in the big cycle annotation. The paper concludes that LLMs offer a potentially powerful tool for historical analysis, but cautions against over-reliance due to potential biases and the risk of narrative homogenization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper offers a valuable contribution to the emerging field of computational history and the application of LLMs in digital humanities. Its novelty lies in the direct comparison of human and LLM annotations on a specific historical task, providing quantitative evidence of their respective strengths and weaknesses. While previous work has explored the use of LLMs for historical analysis, this paper distinguishes itself by focusing on *consensus* and *bias* in historical *interpretation*, rather than simple information retrieval or question answering.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Research Questions:** The research questions are well-defined and address important concerns regarding the use of LLMs in historical analysis.
*   **Rigorous Methodology:** The experimental design is well-structured, including the use of established theoretical frameworks (SDT and Big Cycle), a curated dataset (Chronos), and standard evaluation metrics (Fleiss' Kappa, correlation coefficient).
*   **Interesting Findings:** The findings are insightful, revealing both the potential of LLMs and the pitfalls of relying on them without careful consideration of biases and errors. The observation that LLMs can achieve higher consensus than humans on certain tasks is particularly noteworthy.
*   **Practical Implications:** The paper discusses the practical implications of its findings for digital humanities, including opportunities for large-scale annotation, interactive education, and the risks of narrative homogenization.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Bias Analysis:** The analysis of historical bias is somewhat limited. While the example of interpreting the 1960s in China is informative, a more in-depth exploration of different cultural perspectives and their impact on annotation would strengthen the paper.  Also, it is worth exploring how prompts in non-Eurocentric languages are compared against Eurocentric languages.
*   **Annotator Demographics:**  The human annotators (Italian students) bring a specific cultural lens to the task, which might affect the baseline for bias comparison.  Having annotators from diverse backgrounds would provide a more robust assessment of the LLMs' bias relative to human perspectives. Also, the small number of annotators (3 per task) limits the generalization of the human annotation baseline.
*   **Oversimplification of Historical Interpretation:** The annotation tasks, while relevant, inevitably simplify the complexities of historical interpretation. Reducing historical events to a single label can be reductionist, and the paper could acknowledge this limitation more explicitly.
*   **Lack of Explainability:** While the authors analyze errors, a deeper dive into why certain LLMs exhibit biases or skip phases could benefit from more explainability techniques to determine which parts of the models are influencing the results.
* **Dependence on Chronos Dataset:** Using only the Chronos dataset poses a risk for "data leakage" during the training of the models, which might inflate performance metrics.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance lies in its contribution to a critical evaluation of LLMs as tools for historical research. It highlights the need for careful consideration of bias, error patterns, and the potential for narrative homogenization when using LLMs to analyze historical data. The findings provide valuable insights for researchers, educators, and policymakers who are interested in leveraging the power of AI for historical understanding, while remaining mindful of its limitations.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed study that offers valuable insights into the use of LLMs for historical interpretation. The methodology is rigorous, the findings are interesting, and the implications are significant for the field of digital humanities. However, the limitations regarding the limited scope of bias analysis, annotator demographics, and oversimplification of historical interpretation prevent it from receiving a higher score. A more in-depth analysis of bias, a more diverse group of human annotators, and a more nuanced discussion of the complexities of historical interpretation would significantly strengthen the paper and increase its impact. Given these limitations, the score reflects the paper's significant contribution balanced with areas for potential improvement.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Rethinking RL Scaling for Vision Language Models: A Transparent, From-Scratch Framework and Comprehensive Evaluation Scheme
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02587v1)
- **Authors**: Yan Ma, Steffi Chern, Xuyang Shen, Yiran Zhong, Pengfei Liu
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning (RL) has recently shown strong potential in improving the reasoning capabilities of large language models and is now being actively extended to vision-language models (VLMs). However, existing RL applications in VLMs often rely on heavily engineered frameworks that hinder reproducibility and accessibility, while lacking standardized evaluation protocols, making it difficult to compare results or interpret training dynamics. This work introduces a transparent, from-scratch framework for RL in VLMs, offering a minimal yet functional four-step pipeline validated across multiple models and datasets. In addition, a standardized evaluation scheme is proposed to assess training dynamics and reflective behaviors. Extensive experiments on visual reasoning tasks uncover key empirical findings: response length is sensitive to random seeds, reflection correlates with output length, and RL consistently outperforms supervised fine-tuning (SFT) in generalization, even with high-quality data. These findings, together with the proposed framework, aim to establish a reproducible baseline and support broader engagement in RL-based VLM research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Rethinking RL Scaling for Vision Language Models: A Transparent, From-Scratch Framework and Comprehensive Evaluation Scheme" addresses the challenges in applying Reinforcement Learning (RL) to Vision-Language Models (VLMs). It proposes a transparent, from-scratch RL framework (MAYE) for VLMs, built using standard libraries and validated across multiple models and datasets. The framework implements a four-step pipeline (data flow, response collection, trajectory generation, and policy update). Additionally, the paper introduces a standardized evaluation scheme to assess RL training dynamics, going beyond single-score evaluations by capturing training dynamics and reflective behaviors through metrics like accuracy curves, response length, and reflection ratio. Through experiments on visual reasoning tasks, the paper uncovers findings regarding response length sensitivity, correlation between reflection and length, and RL's superior generalization compared to Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT).  The code for the framework is publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary contribution lies in providing a transparent and reproducible framework for RL in VLMs. While RL application to VLMs isn't entirely new, the "from-scratch" implementation, using only standard libraries, offers significant accessibility and clarity compared to existing complex RL libraries. The paper fills a gap by demystifying RL implementation, making it easier for researchers to understand, replicate, and modify the training process.  The standardized evaluation scheme, focusing on training dynamics beyond final performance, is also a valuable contribution.  The empirical findings, particularly regarding the interplay of length, reflection, and generalization, provide actionable insights.

* **Significance:** The paper's significance rests on its potential to democratize research in RL for VLMs. By providing a transparent baseline, the MAYE framework lowers the barrier to entry for researchers, promoting broader engagement and potentially accelerating innovation. The standardized evaluation scheme addresses a critical need for reliable and comparable results in the field. The empirical findings help researchers understand the dynamics of RL training in VLMs.  The emphasis on reproducible results addresses an important concern within the broader machine learning community.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Transparency and Reproducibility:** The "from-scratch" approach is a major strength, facilitating understanding and modification.
    * **Standardized Evaluation:** The evaluation scheme offers a more nuanced assessment of RL training than typical single-score reports.
    * **Empirical Insights:** The observations on length, reflection, and generalization are valuable contributions.
    * **Code Availability:** Publicly available code promotes reproducibility and further research.
    * **Well-Structured and Clearly Written:** The paper is easy to follow and understand.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Performance Optimization:** The authors acknowledge that the framework isn't optimized for state-of-the-art performance, focusing instead on transparency.  While this is a deliberate choice, it limits its immediate applicability for those solely seeking maximal performance.
    * **Limited Algorithmic Exploration:** While an alternative RL algorithm (Reinforce++) is used, the choice is not thoroughly justified against commonly used algorithms such as PPO.
    * **Narrow Task Focus:** The experiments are limited to visual reasoning tasks involving mathematical problems. While this allows for verifiable answers, it reduces the generalizability of conclusions to other VLM tasks.
    * **Increment in performance for a state of the art model such as Qwen 2.5 is limited**

* **Potential Impact:** The paper can establish a common ground for future RL-VLM research. Researchers are able to base their research on top of the framework. The insights contribute to a more thorough understanding of how RL impacts VLMs.

* **Justification for Score:**

The paper offers a significant contribution by promoting transparency and reproducibility in RL for VLMs.  While the framework is not designed for SOTA performance and the experimental tasks are somewhat narrow, the focus on accessibility, clear implementation, and a robust evaluation scheme outweigh these limitations. The empirical insights contribute to a better understanding of VLM training dynamics. Therefore, this paper is significant and will contribute to the understanding of RL-VLMs.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Multi-SWE-bench: A Multilingual Benchmark for Issue Resolving
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02605v1)
- **Authors**: Daoguang Zan, Zhirong Huang, Wei Liu, Hanwu Chen, Linhao Zhang, Shulin Xin, Lu Chen, Qi Liu, Xiaojian Zhong, Aoyan Li, Siyao Liu, Yongsheng Xiao, Liangqiang Chen, Yuyu Zhang, Jing Su, Tianyu Liu, Rui Long, Kai Shen, Liang Xiang
- **Abstract**: The task of issue resolving is to modify a codebase to generate a patch that addresses a given issue. However, existing benchmarks, such as SWE-bench, focus almost exclusively on Python, making them insufficient for evaluating Large Language Models (LLMs) across diverse software ecosystems. To address this, we introduce a multilingual issue-resolving benchmark, called Multi-SWE-bench, covering Java, TypeScript, JavaScript, Go, Rust, C, and C++. It includes a total of 1,632 high-quality instances, which were carefully annotated from 2,456 candidates by 68 expert annotators, ensuring that the benchmark can provide an accurate and reliable evaluation. Based on Multi-SWE-bench, we evaluate a series of state-of-the-art models using three representative methods (Agentless, SWE-agent, and OpenHands) and present a comprehensive analysis with key empirical insights. In addition, we launch a Multi-SWE-RL open-source community, aimed at building large-scale reinforcement learning (RL) training datasets for issue-resolving tasks. As an initial contribution, we release a set of 4,723 well-structured instances spanning seven programming languages, laying a solid foundation for RL research in this domain. More importantly, we open-source our entire data production pipeline, along with detailed tutorials, encouraging the open-source community to continuously contribute and expand the dataset. We envision our Multi-SWE-bench and the ever-growing Multi-SWE-RL community as catalysts for advancing RL toward its full potential, bringing us one step closer to the dawn of AGI.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Multi-SWE-bench, a new multilingual benchmark for the task of issue resolving in software engineering.  Unlike existing benchmarks like SWE-bench, which primarily focus on Python, Multi-SWE-bench covers seven programming languages: Java, TypeScript, JavaScript, Go, Rust, C, and C++. The benchmark consists of 1,632 high-quality, human-validated issue instances carefully selected from open-source repositories. The authors evaluate the performance of several state-of-the-art large language models (LLMs) and software agent approaches on Multi-SWE-bench, providing a comparative analysis of their effectiveness across different languages and issue types.  Furthermore, they launch Multi-SWE-RL, an open-source community initiative aimed at building large-scale reinforcement learning (RL) datasets for issue resolving and release a dataset of 4,723 instances. The paper concludes with a discussion of key challenges, limitations, and future directions in the field of multilingual software agent development.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the creation of a *multilingual* benchmark for issue resolving.  SWE-bench, while valuable, is limited to Python.  Multi-SWE-bench directly addresses this gap by providing a more representative and diverse set of tasks. The construction process is well-defined, employing a rigorous five-phase pipeline to ensure data quality.  The paper goes beyond simply creating a benchmark and also introduces the Multi-SWE-RL community and dataset, which is a significant contribution to facilitating research in RL for code.

*   **Significance:**  The significance of this work stems from its potential to drive advancements in LLM-based software engineering agents. By exposing models to a broader range of languages and programming paradigms, Multi-SWE-bench can help identify limitations in existing approaches and encourage the development of more robust and generalizable solutions.  The comprehensive evaluation of different LLMs and methods provides valuable insights into their strengths and weaknesses, guiding future research directions.  The release of the Multi-SWE-RL dataset and the creation of an open community will accelerate the development of RL-based agents for issue resolving, bringing the field closer to automating complex software engineering tasks.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Multilingual Coverage:** The most significant strength is the benchmark's coverage of multiple programming languages, reflecting the diversity of real-world software ecosystems.
    *   **Rigorous Construction:** The five-phase pipeline, including manual verification and Dockerization, ensures high data quality and reproducibility.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper provides a thorough evaluation of several state-of-the-art LLMs and methods, offering a detailed comparative analysis.
    *   **Community Initiative:** The launch of Multi-SWE-RL and the release of the dataset foster collaboration and accelerate research in RL for code.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The in-depth analysis of various factors influencing model performance (issue type, description length, patch characteristics) provides valuable insights.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Agent Adaptation:** The paper mentions adapting existing methods (Agentless, SWE-agent, OpenHands) for multilingual support, but the modifications seem to be largely focused on prompt engineering.  A more in-depth discussion of how these methods were adapted to handle language-specific intricacies would be beneficial.
    *   **Agent Performance:** Though benchmark is released, the performance of agents on the benchmark isn't satisfactory with best performance at 20%. The complexity of benchmark and models should be aligned for practical application in future.
    *   **Lack of Novel Methods:** The paper focuses on *evaluating* existing methods rather than *introducing* novel approaches to issue resolving. While the evaluation is valuable, the absence of a new method limits the paper's direct impact on improving model performance.
    *   **Evaluation Scope:** The evaluation focuses primarily on "Resolved Rate". While this is a standard metric, including other evaluation metrics like "Code Quality" or "Efficiency" could provide a more complete picture of model performance.

*   **Potential Influence:**  Multi-SWE-bench has the potential to become a widely adopted benchmark in the field of LLM-based software engineering. The Multi-SWE-RL initiative could also spur significant advances in RL for code. The paper's insights and analysis will likely influence future research directions, leading to the development of more robust and generalizable software agents.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field by providing a much-needed multilingual benchmark for issue resolving.  While the adaptation of existing methods and lack of new proposed method limits it's novelty, the meticulous data construction, comprehensive evaluation, and community-driven aspect make this work significant. The community and RL dataset will also likely inspire a lot of further research.
The potential for Multi-SWE-bench to become a standard benchmark and the impetus of Multi-SWE-RL earn it a high rating.
