- **Score**: 8/10

### Sparse Autoencoders Learn Monosemantic Features in Vision-Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02821v1)
- **Authors**: Mateusz Pach, Shyamgopal Karthik, Quentin Bouniot, Serge Belongie, Zeynep Akata
- **Abstract**: Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs) have recently been shown to enhance interpretability and steerability in Large Language Models (LLMs). In this work, we extend the application of SAEs to Vision-Language Models (VLMs), such as CLIP, and introduce a comprehensive framework for evaluating monosemanticity in vision representations. Our experimental results reveal that SAEs trained on VLMs significantly enhance the monosemanticity of individual neurons while also exhibiting hierarchical representations that align well with expert-defined structures (e.g., iNaturalist taxonomy). Most notably, we demonstrate that applying SAEs to intervene on a CLIP vision encoder, directly steer output from multimodal LLMs (e.g., LLaVA) without any modifications to the underlying model. These findings emphasize the practicality and efficacy of SAEs as an unsupervised approach for enhancing both the interpretability and control of VLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a framework for evaluating and improving the monosemanticity of features learned by Vision-Language Models (VLMs) using Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs).  It extends the application of SAEs, which have shown promise in enhancing interpretability and steerability in Large Language Models (LLMs), to VLMs like CLIP. The core contribution is the development of a Monosemanticity Score (MS) that quantifies how consistently a neuron fires for semantically similar images. The authors demonstrate that training SAEs on VLMs increases the monosemanticity of individual neurons.  They show that these SAE-enhanced representations exhibit hierarchical structures aligning with expert-defined taxonomies (e.g., iNaturalist). Furthermore, they demonstrate that intervening on the SAE's activations can steer the output of multimodal LLMs (MLLMs), even without modifying the underlying MLLM parameters.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The application of SAEs to VLMs isn't entirely novel, as prior works have explored SAEs for interpreting CLIP features. However, the paper makes significant contributions in several areas. The Monosemanticity Score (MS) provides a new, quantifiable measure of neuron interpretability specifically tailored for vision tasks. The systematic evaluation of SAE architectures (BatchTopK, Matryoshka) and hyperparameter tuning (sparsity levels, expansion factors) is a more comprehensive analysis than has been previously performed. The most innovative aspect is the demonstration of using SAEs for *steering* multimodal LLMs (MLLMs) by intervening in the CLIP vision encoder, allowing control over MLLM outputs without retraining the LLM. While the steering concept is not entirely new in LLMs, its application via SAEs in VLMs with the observed steering of MLLMs represents an innovative combination.
* **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial challenge in VLMs: understanding and controlling their internal representations. Enhancing monosemanticity has implications for model interpretability, debugging, and safe deployment. The ability to steer MLLM outputs through SAE intervention could enable new functionalities, such as targeted content generation or bias mitigation.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear and Well-Defined Metric:** The MS provides a practical way to assess monosemanticity in vision tasks.
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper investigates different SAE architectures and hyperparameters, providing valuable insights into their impact on monosemanticity.
    * **Practical Demonstration:** The MLLM steering experiments are compelling and demonstrate the potential of SAEs for controlling VLM behavior.
    * **Detailed Experimental Setup:** The experimental setup is well-described, enhancing reproducibility.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Incremental Improvement:** While the MLLM steering is compelling, the degree of fine-grained control may still be limited. The results of MS improvement may not have direct impacts on the end task performance.
    * **Limited Scope of MLLM Steering:** The MLLM steering examples are limited to a few specific prompts. A broader range of interventions and a more quantitative assessment of the steering effect would strengthen the findings.
   * **Dependency on CLIP encoder:** The steering depends on specific CLIP encoder. A more comprehensive study of robustness by choosing different encoders may be warranted.
* **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in VLM interpretability, steerability, and control. The MS could become a standard metric for evaluating monosemanticity in vision representations. The steering approach could inspire new methods for controlling MLLM behavior in various applications.

**Justification:**

The paper has some key strengths, the most significant being the ability to steer the VLMs and MLLMs with MS metric defined for neurons in SAE. While the novelty of SAE's application in VLMs is somewhat limited due to existing work, the comprehensive evaluation and the MLLM steering experiments are significant advances. Considering these factors, a score of 7 reflects the paper's significant contribution while acknowledging the weaknesses and areas for improvement.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10
